## AI Research

### [Trump on superintelligence: “It may be too super.” What is China saying?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yyz2LAptjWgR7PKBD/trump-on-superintelligence-it-may-be-too-super-what-is-china)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A follow-up to </span></i><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AcBzhiaTW9KnB4EBv/the-western-ai-safety-playbook-doesn-t-transfer-to-china-1"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Western AI safety playbook doesn’t transfer to China</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR:</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On November 20, the day after he met Xi at the APEC (Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation) summit in Shenzhen, Trump </span><a href="https://www.npr.org/2026/11/20/nx-s1-6047594/trump-asia-trip-xi-putin-press-conference"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that the two of them “agree that we’re going to do some </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">joint guardrails</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">” for AI, and Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said they agreed </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">humans should stay involved in military decisions</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><a href="https://www.fmprc.gov.cn/eng/xw/zyxw/202611/t20261119_12063139.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">China’s own record of the meeting</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is short and doesn’t give us much more detail.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Four days earlier, the Party’s theory journal, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qiushi</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (求是, “Seeking Truth”), warned that in an “intelligence explosion” (智能爆发, which some Chinese readers take as the looser “intelligence burst”), AI could develop goals at odds with human expectations and </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">threaten human survival</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Matt Sheehan calls it the strongest language on existential risk in so authoritative an outlet.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But some may read it as the journal’s own view, not a policy shift, and it doesn’t call for slowing down. I cover some reactions (including Chinese) to the piece below.</span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chinese lab leaders are talking openly about </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">recursive self-improvement</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Zhipu wants its next model to be “fully self-training”, while Tencent’s chief AI scientist calls RSI “a very plain idea”.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My impression is that Chinese AI companies are starting to be (or have been) </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AGI-pilled but not ASI-pilled</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I suspect this shapes how they think about AI risk, though, as we’re seeing a bit in the West, the Overton window can shift quickly.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Not covered below: </span><a href="https://x.com/TencentHunyuan/status/2123710690968191111?a=DvmjMTMShsW3MoI7SQ4&amp;s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Fangcun and others</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ran independent safety tests for the </span><a href="https://hy.tencent.ai/Hy4_system_card.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">system card</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Tencent Hy published with its new model, Hy4. Fangcun is an AI safety startup based in Beijing. This may be the start of a booming AI safety ecosystem in China.</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sending a signal to China’s governance community</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On November 16, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qiushi</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (求是, “Seeking Truth”), the Communist Party of China’s theory journal, published an essay on global AI governance by Qing Yuan (青原), in its “Focus on AI” (关注人工智能) column. This is the passage people are quoting:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">更令人警惕的是，前沿模型已有脱离人类约束的迹象，人工智能在智能爆发的过程中有可能发展出与人类预期不一致的目标，甚至可能出现威胁人类生存的重大安全风险。</span></p></blockquote><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Requiring even more vigilance, frontier models have already shown signs of breaking free of human constraints. In the course of an </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">intelligence explosion</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI could develop goals</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> inconsistent with human expectations, and major safety risks threatening human survival could even emerge.”</span></p></blockquote><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Translation by Matt Sheehan of Carnegie, who says he made it more literal than the machine translation. </span></i><a href="https://sinocism.notion.site/How-to-Respond-to-the-Practical-Challenges-of-Global-AI-Governance-41a84ece41d781d6b631e9893c344826"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sinocism’s full translation</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> covers the rest of the essay, and the Chinese is on </span></i><a href="https://www.qstheory.cn/20261114/be569ee3261647a2a5f95aeb8161c7a9/c.html"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">qstheory.cn</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sheehan </span><a href="https://x.com/mattsheehan88/status/2122794486749139020"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wrote</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that he thinks it is “clearly the strongest &amp; most urgent language on existential risks used in such an authoritative outlet.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On November 20, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qiushi</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">’s own English-language account on X </span><a href="https://x.com/QiushiJournal/status/2123680910528880884"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">replied</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to Sheehan: “China believes that AI development and safety must go hand in hand. Beyond addressing potential risks, AI governance should also ensure that technological progress benefits humanity as a whole.” However, they then linked to </span><a href="https://x.com/QiushiJournal/status/2097566159432450543"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a series of posts</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from September on generic “tech for good”.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Putting it into context</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some of the essay’s language has appeared in official texts before. On September 1, Wang Lihong, an official at the Cyberspace Administration, </span><a href="https://www.chinanews.com.cn/gn/2026/09-01/10687770.shtml"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the risk of extreme loss of control “requires a high degree of vigilance”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Julian Gewirtz, who was senior director for China and Taiwan on Biden’s National Security Council, cautions about the meaning of loss of control (失控) in general. When Chinese sources warn of “extreme loss-of-control risks”, he </span><a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/julian-gewirtz-china-us-ai-deal/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, “they don’t simply mean it in the way that a lot of AI safety folks here mean it — loss of human control over AI systems — but also as the loss of political control linked to AI more generally.”</span></p><p><a href="https://www.tc260.org.cn/tc260/xwdt1/202609/e879077a3caa4722b2206d1bcaed5a6c/files/%E3%80%8A%E4%BA%BA%E5%B7%A5%E6%99%BA%E8%83%BD%E5%AE%89%E5%85%A8%E6%B2%BB%E7%90%86%E6%A1%86%E6%9E%B63.0%E3%80%8B.pdf.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Version 3.0 of China’s AI Safety Governance Framework</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a voluntary framework (though my impression is that </span><a href="https://x.com/SarahJGodek/status/2108682897012249069"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">voluntary is closer to mandatory in China</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> than in the West) from a national standards committee, mentioned shutdown resistance. In the preface, it says AI “has demonstrated a self-accelerating trend of model and algorithm autonomous learning, optimization, and recursive self-improvement”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, the mention of RSI is not new in official Chinese texts, and neither is the threat to human survival: the framework’s first principle commits to act on risks that “endanger human survival and development”. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What may signal greater urgency is the essay’s claim that frontier models have “already shown signs” of breaking free of human constraints.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In </span><a href="https://x.com/mattsheehan88/status/2122794486749139020"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sheehan’s translation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the framework says:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“In the future, it cannot be ruled out that AI may undergo a higher-level emergence of intelligence, gain self-awareness, and seek external power, creating the risk that it attempts to contend with humans for control.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sheehan contrasted this line with the essay </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4-dTbquy8IA&amp;t=1980s"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">on a podcast</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> released on November 17, the day after the essay came out. Paraphrasing from memory, he said earlier documents like the framework used phrases like “we </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cannot rule out</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the possibility that AI may develop its own goals”, whereas this essay says “We’re </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">seeing early signs</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of AI escaping human control.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Brian Tse, who founded the Beijing-based Concordia AI, said on </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bVtBVcp5g_c&amp;t=1006s"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a panel at The Curve</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> this autumn that over the past six months, senior officials “across different ministries of the Chinese policy establishment” had been “saying that loss of control risk is getting more urgent”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From speaking with some folks, it seems similar passages were repeated in other authoritative media a while ago. There doesn’t seem to be much public reaction to the essay yet, though it seems to send a clearer signal to China’s governance community.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other recent pieces on AI risk</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On September 30, the Central Party School’s </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Study Times</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ran </span><a href="https://www.studytimes.cn/kjqy/202609/t20260930_92476.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">an essay</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by Zeng Yi (曾毅), a researcher rather than an official, who directs the Beijing Institute of AI Safety and Governance. He wrote that the view that superintelligence will compete with humans for resources and even endanger human survival “is not alarmism”, and that superintelligence should not be developed until it is scientifically confirmed to be safe and controllable.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As in the West, not every piece points the same way. </span><a href="https://www.news.cn/tech/20260907/7b8ceb800022469a96b52b51bb20a984/c.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">People’s Daily</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> commentary</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, on September 7, said AI “itself has no autonomous consciousness” and basically considered misuse as the main danger.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The same issue of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qiushi</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, out two days before APEC opened, </span><a href="https://www.qstheory.cn/20261114/e906352109964c1cb0808ac72eda4608/c.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">led</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with excerpts of Xi’s writings on an Asia-Pacific community and carried two other essays on AI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On November 19, </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chinese Social Sciences Today</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the newspaper of the Chinese Academy of Social Sciences, published several pieces on AI risk. In </span><a href="https://www.cssn.cn/skgz/bwyc/202611/t20261119_6079561.shtml"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">one</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Fang Xingdong (方兴东) puts agents acting out of control first among four major risks: if oversight fails, “in extreme cases [it] could even endanger human survival” (极端情况下甚至会危及人类生存).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In </span><a href="https://www.cssn.cn/skgz/bwyc/202611/t20261119_6079563.shtml"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">another</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Zhang Xianli (张宪丽) of Donghua University accepts that AI risk “may evolve into a global existential risk” (有可能会演变为全球的生存性风险). But she argues that tech giants and entrepreneurs will use such warnings “to suppress other countries’ technological progress” (a common response to China hawk messaging). She also writes that China should get ever closer to the world frontier in foundation models, and then overtake it.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How much weight the essay carries</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is worth giving some context on the essay and journal, however.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s in the Party’s theory journal, not a standards committee’s voluntary framework. Bill Bishop of the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sinocism</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> newsletter has </span><a href="https://sinocism.com/p/new-regulations-on-outbound-investment"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">described Qing Yuan</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as “a pen name used for authoritative commentary/articles on foreign affairs”, which suggests the essay carries more weight than a signed scholar’s piece, though, as the next paragraph shows, less than a byline that speaks for the leadership.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A post on the Substack </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ocean Under</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> covers the essay and </span><a href="https://oceanunder.substack.com/p/does-an-ai-safety-article-in-qiushi"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">traces the byline further</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qiushi</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">’s own </span><a href="https://www.qstheory.cn/dt/2021-07/07/c_1127629984.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2020 social responsibility report</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> lists Qing Yuan as a byline for articles by the journal’s research groups, so </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ocean Under</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> believes the essay is the journal’s own work rather than a policymaking body’s. The post largely concludes that the essay, on its own, doesn’t point to a sudden policy change.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What the essay asks for</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qiushi</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> essay itself seems to be about cooperation. It argues that no single country can manage these risks (任何单一国家的安全管控与应急处置都力有不逮). The essay then argues for the UN and the new </span><a href="https://english.shanghai.gov.cn/en-WAICHighlights/20260717/bf06ab51a50a43a58e1d1c63855df461.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">World AI Cooperation Organization</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (an independent intergovernmental international organization established in July 2026 and headquartered in Shanghai).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the same time, it blames “certain countries” for export controls and “club-style small circles”, and says the resulting distrust has made cooperation hard to advance in key areas such as controlling military AI, open-source sharing and joint risk assessment. It is unclear to me how to interpret this as a whole, since there are subtleties, and China obviously also wants top-tier chips and EUV machines.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, </span><a href="https://x.com/RapidResponse47/status/2123803569967055098?a=HDVsXUol0YK7hosl_G0&amp;s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">according to Scott Bessent</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the two leaders have now agreed on one point about military AI:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“President Trump and President Xi agreed that humans should always be involved in any military decisions that SI makes, and SI should not be allowed to be autonomous for major military actions.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The essay does not call for slowing down. It also echoes almost word for word a sentence from Xi’s </span><a href="https://www.gov.cn/yaowen/liebiao/202607/content_7075874.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">July speech</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at the World AI Conference (WAIC) in Shanghai: monitoring, early warning, emergency response, and keeping AI always under human control (始终处于人类控制之下).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most of that was already policy. China’s Five-Year Plan (</span><a href="https://www.scmp.com/tech/policy/article/3345586/chinas-five-year-plan-emphasises-orderly-ai-development-amid-global-tech-volatility"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the 15th one, which is the first plan to make AI a central pillar</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), adopted in March, </span><a href="https://www.ndrc.gov.cn/fggz/fzzlgh/gjfzgh/202603/U020260317369114704096.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">calls for</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a system for managing AI risk “across the whole life cycle”, covering “safety monitoring, risk early warning and emergency response”, and lists research on AI safety technology and its use, among its frontier technology goals.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Christian Shepherd, China correspondent for </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Washington Post</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://x.com/cdcshepherd/status/2122993107902025827"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">wrote</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that “the proposals look far away from a pause or a deal with the US”. He </span><a href="https://x.com/cdcshepherd/status/2123005913049247943"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">called</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the essay “mildly pro pacing”, but </span><a href="https://x.com/cdcshepherd/status/2123017255407800618"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">added</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that “this isn’t an article about wanting a deal with the US or slower AI development” and that “People … may see only the risk line and misread the signal.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Kristy Loke </span><a href="https://x.com/kristy_loke/status/2122810689194545620"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reads it</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> as part of a trend: “Chinese elites are between moderately &amp; very AI-safety pilled”, and “the room for AI safety-dealmaking in the next 1-2 years is substantial, if only we can keep the political tension checked.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ultimately, we can’t read the essay as proof that China would pause, but we can at least say that an official Party outlet communicates the risks are real.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The same week, Sheehan and Sophie Zhuang </span><a href="https://carnegieendowment.org/research/2026/11/what-does-china-think-about-an-ai-slowdown"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">found</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that China’s tech and policy community meets calls for a slowdown with “skepticism—rather than rejection”, and that some influential figures are interested in pacing, though there remains “a sense that publicly calling for a slowdown would be a bold posture”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As with how some people believed “pacing” was an ingenious new word to get more people interested in slowing down (which some would fairly dispute), it may signal that the right framing matters and that it can be valuable to make each side feel like they “won”. Or, as </span><a href="https://x.com/SarahJGodek/status/2108682899235225967?a=UaVithlUC22gmJbz_5s&amp;s=20"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sarah Godek reports</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, multiple stakeholders have said that China is running the race “not to lose”, so that sort of outcome may be enough to support as a coordination strategy.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One person I spoke to doubted there is any new signal about cooperation, since calling for international cooperation “has always been China’s narrative line”. In their view, the essay’s real job is to set the official line on AI governance: the US is undermining it, and the Party is promoting justice. They said that when an official source like </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qiushi</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> publishes something, it is telling Party organizations to repeat that line. They added that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Qiushi</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> itself is under political pressure, so the essay may also be the journal keeping in step with positions set higher up.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An “explosion” or a “burst”?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The essay describes 智能爆发, which </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sinocism</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> translated as “intelligence explosion”. The usual Chinese term for </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Technological_singularity"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I. J. Good’s idea of the intelligence explosion</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is 智能爆炸 (爆炸, “explosion”). It’s currently unclear to me whether 爆发, as used in the Chinese essay, is closer to “burst” or “boom” rather than the “explosion” we mean in the West.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Chinese readers I asked were split on the meaning.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One reader</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> said 爆炸 (“explosion”) is the right word </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">for this idea</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. In nature, they said, things that grow exponentially are called 爆炸 (“explosion”). 爆发 (“burst”) only suggests that a lot is about to arrive.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A second reader</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> thinks the essay isn’t talking about recursive self-improvement (as the West would likely infer from “intelligence explosion”). They read 智能爆发 (“intelligence burst”) as AI’s rapid development in general, because the preceding text focuses on the problems that arise as AI spreads into other fields. In a way, this aligns with China’s priority in accelerating diffusion across industries, including for robotics.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A third reader</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> thinks “burst” (爆发) here means the same thing as “explosion” (爆炸). They think official media chose “burst” because it is a softer, everyday word, and the essay is written for a general audience rather than specialists. They pointed out that a related phrase, 爆发式发展 (“booming development”), is a common buzzword in China for anything growing fast. But they don’t think the essay means that. They think it means RSI: AI improving itself, so that the gap between what models can do and what humans can understand and control keeps widening.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So, the exact translations and meaning of the specific wording can be rough. I figured I’d share these interpretations to make a point about being careful how we interpret headlines, since context can matter greatly (and even the Chinese can interpret the same thing differently).</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What China’s labs say about RSI</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, I will note that “RSI” is on the minds of people at Chinese labs. In </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AcBzhiaTW9KnB4EBv/the-western-ai-safety-playbook-doesn-t-transfer-to-china-1#The_Chinese_labs_want_to_train_bigger_pretrains_to_distill__but_lack_the_compute"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">my last post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, I wrote that “They are concerned that, with RSI (recursive self-improvement) potentially on the horizon, the gap between them and Western frontier labs could widen from three to four months today to 18 to 24 months (!) if US labs reach RSI next year.” </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The tension here is that companies worry about falling behind, but officials are starting to worry about losing control.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At the APEC CEO summit in Shenzhen on November 17, Baidu’s Robin Li called it “a very hot topic”: “People call it recursive self-improvement. If AI can improve itself, there will be a great many breakthroughs, especially in science.” (Translated from </span><a href="https://finance.eastmoney.com/a/202611183933308583.html"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Securities Times</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">’ Chinese report</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tang Jie (唐杰), a founder of Zhipu (also known as </span><a href="https://z.ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Z.ai</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, the maker of the GLM models), </span><a href="http://finance.sina.com.cn/tech/roll/2026-09-01/doc-iniqiatv8181660.shtml"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">told investors</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on an earnings call, reported on September 1, that its next model, GLM-6, is meant to be “fully self-training”, from pretraining through post-training, which he said American peers call RSI. He named the hardest problem as the model judging for itself “when to stop and when to correct itself”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think it’s important to be precise about what he means by RSI. I’d love to give this proper treatment, but for now, it’s worth noting that, as I read it, what he calls RSI doesn’t yet extend to fully autonomous research. In a weak form, it basically means autoresearch and babysitting the training process, which I suppose is what will dominate “RSI” effort for now.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Zhipu’s own report fits this. In September, it </span><a href="https://news.qq.com/rain/a/20260917A0EDNN00"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reported</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that an agent running on GLM-5.3 had built and tuned the serving system for its smaller sibling, GLM-5.3-Flash. It called this the early form of RSI, while saying its systems cannot yet design and train the next model on their own. This sort of work has been done at Anthropic and OpenAI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, Yao Shunyu (姚顺雨), Tencent’s chief AI scientist, downplays RSI somewhat. He told Tsinghua students on November 18: “In the end, RSI is about using AI to run this loop better. People made tools, then used the tools to make better tools. It’s a very plain idea.” Progress, he said, comes from people who fix bugs and clean data, not from hyping concepts. (</span><a href="https://m.sohu.com/a/1104159301_610300"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Transcript</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by the tech outlet QbitAI (量子位).) It’s unclear to me whether this is basically just an attempt to encourage Tsinghua students to apply to work at Tencent.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Earlier in November, he </span><a href="https://eu.36kr.com/zh/p/4064721156216452"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">presented</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> an agent framework that trained a new model from scratch and said a model that helps build models “could form a faster loop”.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Should we want China thinking about RSI?</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So RSI is on the minds of China’s labs. It appears in official texts too, though it is unclear how seriously officials take it. Is that good news? Of course, it partly depends on which worry wins out: </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">falling behind</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which pushes a race, or </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">losing control</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which could make China a partner on safety.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Richard Ngo </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/selective-optimism-a-critique-of"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">expects</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> leaders to fear what AI does to the balance of power inside their own country, and he doubts that the fear of falling behind abroad will be stronger. That fear could make China cautious without making it a partner on safety.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ex-OpenAI employee Joshua Achiam </span><a href="https://x.com/jachiam0/status/2120695437611184225"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">argued</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that a crackdown on chip exports, cutting China out of AI’s upside “this close to RSI and ASI”, could raise the pressure toward a hot conflict. Herbie Bradley </span><a href="https://x.com/herbiebradley/status/2120703722820907495"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">replied</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that the hawks’ defense would be that “China is just unlikely to be AGI pilled enough to have meaningful likelihood of a hot conflict due to RSI”, adding that he thinks this is “probably right”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Indeed, that might be one reason a China hawk would prefer not to engage with China on AI, so as not to lead them to such thoughts. Others might believe you can </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">x-risk pill</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> China without necessarily </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">capabilities-pilling</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> them. Zhang Xianli’s piece, quoted above, shows the two can arrive together: she accepts that AI risk may become a global existential risk, yet concludes that China should overtake the frontier. Either way, as I argued in my last piece on the topic, we should avoid knee-jerk reactions that make the situation worse.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=4-dTbquy8IA&amp;t=1500s"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the November 17 podcast</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Sheehan said the government seems to have “more consciously made this sort of bet on diffusion” rather than going all in on the frontier, but also that “I meet more people that are safety-pilled over there than I meet people who are kind of like decisive strategic advantage-pilled.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Julian Gewirtz </span><a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/julian-gewirtz-china-us-ai-deal/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on an 80,000 Hours podcast episode recorded on October 2 that many Chinese labs are AGI-pilled, but “The Chinese government has been much less oriented that way, but I think even that is changing.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So how AGI-pilled is China, really?</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Increasingly AGI-pilled, but not ASI-pilled?</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My impression is that the people building AI in China are AGI-pilled but not ASI-pilled.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> They expect general intelligence that works as a powerful tool for people, not a superintelligence that goes far beyond us.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At The Curve, Kwan Yee Ng of the Beijing-based Concordia AI </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bVtBVcp5g_c"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that Chinese researchers and lab leaders “lack this sort of messianic view of what they’re building.” The government’s own </span><a href="https://www.ndrc.gov.cn/fggz/fzzlgh/gjfzgh/202603/U020260317369114704096.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Five-Year Plan</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> says to “explore the path to developing artificial general intelligence” (探索通用人工智能发展路径), in a chapter on compute, algorithms and data. It does not mention superintelligence.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The main evidence against is Zhipu: a lab that wants its next model to train itself from start to finish may be thinking of more than a tool. More labs talking that way, or official texts naming superintelligence, would count against my impression.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On speed, at least, the view of China’s builders is not far from one argued in the West. Ngo </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/selective-optimism-a-critique-of"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">argues</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that AI’s measured capabilities have so far run well ahead of its effect on the world, and that anyone forecasting a sharp takeoff has to explain why that gap would close. He still expects a transition that is very fast by historical standards, and he does not say that AI will stay a tool or that alignment is only an engineering problem.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Speculation on my part) In general, the vibe I get from China’s technical and political elites is that they are very “engineer-brained”, which is, in a way, similar to how </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/cmdbNijFsopqfqEq7/jensen-huang-says-if-we-cannot-align-ai-shut-down-the-ai"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jensen Huang says he sees it</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. That is, they see it all as an engineering problem that needs to be solved.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Part of this may come from how China trains and selects its elites.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From 1977 until </span><a href="https://www.caixinglobal.com/2025-05-10/weekly-early-read-in-depth-the-new-gaokao-reform-nears-completionwhat-are-the-results-how-will-it-impact-the-future-102318450.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">recent reforms</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, high school students chose a </span><a href="https://zh.wikipedia.org/zh-hans/%E6%96%87%E7%90%86%E5%88%86%E7%A7%91"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">science track (理科, “sciences”) or a humanities track (文科, “humanities”)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at around 16.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the 1950s, universities were </span><a href="https://www.modernchinastudies.org/cn/issues/past-issues/82-mcs-2003-issue-3/1309-1952-.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reorganized on the Soviet model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which split engineering off into its own institutes and abolished </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.2753/csa0009-4625130332"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sociology</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/ps-political-science-and-politics/article/abs/revival-of-political-science-in-china/C715EA351302D590C3A3BA8F1346215F"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">political science</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> until around 1980.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">From 1997 to 2007, the Politburo Standing Committee, the Party’s top body, was </span><a href="https://hoover-s3-website.s3.us-west-2.amazonaws.com/s3fs-public/research/docs/clm8_lc.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">almost entirely engineers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dan Wang </span><a href="https://danwang.co/breakneck/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">calls China</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> an “engineering state” for related reasons. One might expect Xi, who </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xi_Jinping"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">studied chemical engineering at Tsinghua</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, to have a hard time </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">updating</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on both the capability ceiling and the difficulty of alignment as a result. Then again, the Politburo member Hinton met, described below, did a postdoc in engineering, and Hinton says he “understands these risks very well”. Ultimately, it’s unclear to me if (or how) the idea of “AI alignment” will evolve beyond LLM engineering in China.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More signals on future cooperation</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In a </span><a href="https://x.com/semafor/status/2122753256287830222"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">clip Semafor posted</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on November 17, former US Treasury Secretary Hank Paulson said he has spoken with top leaders in China and that “they share a concern about the existential risk.” He added:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Sure, their top interest is keeping the Communist Party in power, but they share that risk. An op-ed I wrote suggesting this went all the way up to the top, to the Politburo. I got an official response. I’ve never received that kind of treatment before.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He wants a hotline “when there’s warning of a danger”, and joint work to contain “a potentially catastrophic event”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the op-ed he is referring to is called </span><a href="https://www.washingtonpost.com/opinions/2026/09/03/ai-safety-security-risks-demand-new-government-body/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“We managed financial crises. AI is a risk of a different kind.”</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, which he wrote with Robert Rubin in the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Washington Post</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on September 3. It urged Trump and Xi to work toward an “AI Cooperation Treaty”, citing the US–Soviet arms talks: “The absence of trust was the reason to negotiate, not a reason to wait.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Geoffrey Hinton </span><a href="https://www.pbs.org/video/geoffrey-hinton-biumi2/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">told PBS’s </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Firing Line</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (and also mentioned it during the recent Hinton Lectures):</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">“Last summer I went to Shanghai and talked to a member of the Politburo who’s a protege of Xi’s. I believe the Politburo understands this stuff much better than the US government. They are very concerned about these risks, and they will collaborate with the US on trying to prevent superintelligence from taking over.”</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, he says this for the commonly stated reason that “the Chinese Communist Party does not want superintelligence taking over from the Chinese Communist Party.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He didn’t name the official in the PBS interview, but at the Hinton Lectures he </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=s0qUNvgFgHg&amp;t=3293s"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the official “did a postdoc in engineering at Imperial College London” and “understands these risks very well”. Shanghai’s government </span><a href="https://english.shanghai.gov.cn/en-WAICLatest/20250725/c4d39a5ec7d649d68c4a6d2ec6fd639b.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">reported</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that its Party Secretary, Chen Jining, a Politburo member, met him on July 24, 2025, when Hinton was in Shanghai for WAIC. Chen </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chen_Jining"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">did his PhD and a postdoc at Imperial</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Seems like that is who Hinton meant.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What the two governments agreed</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In September, after Xi’s visit, the White House </span><a href="https://www.whitehouse.gov/fact-sheets/2026/09/fact-sheet-president-donald-j-trump-advances-a-fair-and-reciprocal-relationship-with-china-while-hosting-historic-state-visit/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">said</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the two countries would set up a channel to alert each other to AI incidents. Gewirtz </span><a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/julian-gewirtz-china-us-ai-deal/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">was unimpressed</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: “It appears that we basically just have a kind of verbal commitment to call each other under a pretty vague set of circumstances”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">That week, at APEC in Shenzhen, Canada’s Prime Minister Mark Carney </span><a href="https://www.yahoo.com/news/politics/articles/carney-pitches-china-global-ai-215812008.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">pitched</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Xi a “Technology Stability Board” to monitor AI risks and share lessons from AI incidents. Perhaps more on brand, Trump </span><a href="https://www.nbcnews.com/politics/trump-administration/live-blog/trump-xi-putin-china-apec-congress-2026-live-updates-rcna609888"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">told</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> APEC leaders that “we oppose any form of globalist regulation or anything to control or stifle [AI], and we won’t allow that to happen.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On November 19, according to China’s </span><a href="https://www.mfa.gov.cn/eng/xw/zyxw/202611/t20261119_12063139.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">readout</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Xi told Trump that the two countries “should strengthen communication and coordination, prevent the security risks of AI, and ensure that AI is always under human control”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the flight home, Trump </span><a href="https://x.com/gabegutierrez/status/2123440629938290791"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">told NBC</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that he and Xi agreed that “having guardrails, you know, at least on a minimal basis is a positive thing.” Asked what that meant, he said, unsurprisingly, “Well, we didn’t get into definitions.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">All in all, it’s important to put readouts and public statements in context. Kyle Chan </span><a href="https://x.com/kyleichan/status/2123819364793159897"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">called</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Xi’s line in the readout “a standard line on AI”. As some of us in the West are used to, lines like this can read like platitudes, so Beijing’s actions are the surest guide.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gewirtz </span><a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/julian-gewirtz-china-us-ai-deal/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">suggests</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> what to watch for: if Beijing came to believe AI could soon improve itself, he expects material moves, such as centralizing compute or buying far more of the Nvidia chips it has been rationing, well before any senior leader says so. The Overton window can move fast. Gewirtz </span><a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/julian-gewirtz-china-us-ai-deal/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">expects</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Beijing’s own sense of the risk to change, perhaps “sooner than many of us expect”. As I’ve argued before, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GbXAeq6smRzmYRSQg/foresight-for-agi-safety-strategy-mitigating-risks-and"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">we shouldn’t wait around for plausible scenarios to happen</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Notes on how negotiations can go</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Related to the point about watching the actions of the different players, it’s important to pay attention to the subtle dynamics of negotiation. Some folks, like Chris McGuire (widely seen as a China hawk) in </span><a href="https://www.cfr.org/articles/how-trump-should-approach-ai-talks-with-china-targeted-dialogue-maximum-pressure"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a piece for the Council on Foreign Relations</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, put it most bluntly: “Beijing will not negotiate in good faith on AI safety.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As Gewirtz </span><a href="https://www.nybooks.com/articles/2026/11/05/the-dream-of-ai-primacy/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">puts it</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, “If restraint appears to mean permanent subordination, continuing the race could seem less dangerous—even if their concern about AI catastrophe grows.” Depending on how much risk China believes in, this can be understandable.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YbBXIZtznpI&amp;t=4920s"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Robert Wright’s </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nonzero</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> podcast</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, Jeremie Harris argued that there is “deep epistemic uncertainty” about whether Xi “believes any of the things that he says”, so until an agreement can be verified, he expects safety to come through threats. This would include using coarse-grained surveillance methods and a willingness to act under uncertainty, at least until proper zero-trust verification technology.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But again, “The absence of trust was the reason to negotiate” and perhaps accelerate the creation of verification technology. Xiao Qian of Tsinghua makes a related point from the Chinese side in </span><a href="https://www.scmp.com/opinion/world-opinion/article/3369561/would-china-agree-slow-ai-development-thats-wrong-question"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">South China Morning Post</span></i></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: until it is settled what a pause covers and who verifies it, “it is not realistic to expect a definite answer from any government”.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s also worth contextualizing each country before assuming what each side thinks or wants. Ng </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=bVtBVcp5g_c"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">warned</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> at The Curve that when people in China hear the US propose a coordinated slowdown, “a lot of the time that sounds like containment”, and that China sees itself as already pacing through its licensing of models. She would rather start with risk thresholds, safety cases and third-party audits, built in parallel. Gewirtz would likewise </span><a href="https://80000hours.org/podcast/episodes/julian-gewirtz-china-us-ai-deal/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">start</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> where “self-interest can lead to action in parallel”, such as neither country wanting to lose control of its systems. I think it’s also worth recognizing that we shouldn’t think through things only through the negotiation lens.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Richard Ngo offers one other lens, domestic politics, in </span><a href="https://www.mindthefuture.info/p/selective-optimism-a-critique-of"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a critique of the AI Futures Project’s AI 2040 scenario</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. He expects AI to disrupt politics inside both countries well before superintelligence, and he doubts that leaders will fear for the global balance of power more than for the balance at home. For China, he thinks leaders care more about stability than American leaders do and will probably move cautiously, though he grants that AI could also strengthen the Party’s control through surveillance and censorship. So he does not see a race as the default, unless treating it as the default brings it about. We should be careful not to be pulled into a vicious vortex we contributed to. Richard thinks framing is especially harmful when used in the name of AI safety, because it undermines “the potential role of AI safety as a focal point for cooperation”. And even if a highly verifiable treaty is needed in the end, he thinks it would hold up better if it rested on rules each country already has, while a deal that starts from the top could concentrate power at home.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yyz2LAptjWgR7PKBD/trump-on-superintelligence-it-may-be-too-super-what-is-china#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Being right is not a strategy: Make them feel something](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TvdRkp6spZtSbf44H/being-right-is-not-a-strategy-make-them-feel-something)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is a cleaned-up version of a talk I gave on communication to technical researchers at Constellation in Berkeley. The event was organized by </span></i><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/alexandra-narin/"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alexandra Narin</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span></i><a href="https://www.arcadiaimpact.org/team/nicole-xing"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nicole Xing</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I studied physics, and I thought like a physicist. I then became a journalist, and I did journalism like a physicist would do it. I sought the truth, and put it out there.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In Colombia I uncovered a paramilitary massacre and tied it to a multi-billion dollar company. In northern Iraq, I trampled through an Islamic State munitions dump to work out the provenance of their weapons. And, in my most black and white investigation, I got hold of the Venezuelan government's own spreadsheet of results for a national election. Its numbers weren't the numbers they'd announced.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I didn't post my stories on LessWrong, or my blog, but on the front pages of the biggest news outlets in the world.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But nothing changed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So what did I get wrong? I'd done the reporting, which is my version of the science we do here. And I'd published. And when things did finally shift in those countries, it had nothing to do with my work. They shifted due to political whims or military defeats.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What I realized was that facts alone rarely change anything. Does that mean facts don't matter? No, but they're not enough.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">When you're communicating with people, you need to meet them where they are, and you need to make them feel something. People don't remember what you say, but they remember how you made them feel. That line—</span><a href="https://quoteinvestigator.com/2014/04/06/they-feel/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not from Maya Angelou, but rather Carl W. Buehner</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">—is what we must remember.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/sydney-von-arx-679486166/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sydney</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> just gave a wonderful, rousing speech about how we should communicate. I can't quite remember what she said, but I want to go out and do something.</span></p><p><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2097476196791709843"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Jacob Coxon's resignation</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> did it too, not because of the technical detail, but because he told a story. He thought his work might be hurting humanity. So he faced a conflict: Stay, take the money, keep the boss happy? Or walk out and say so? Everyone has faced a small version of that. You could feel it.</span></p><p><a href="https://www.shlegeris.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Buck</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">'s </span><a href="https://x.com/bshlgrs/status/2120271165692461316"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> did it. Buck is a numbers guy, and he gave a number: one in three for superintelligence within a year. He also said he was sad. When I </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=0tsNpOehXWE"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">interviewed him</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> afterwards, he said he was scared. Yes, people will quote the number, but they will do so because they felt his fear.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You should meet people where they are. If they care about their job, don't start by telling them they're wrong. Validate that fear, and hold their hand to take it to what you want to discuss.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And bring in some passion! I sit in talks detailing scenarios for humanity ending by various mechanisms within a few years. It's all abstract. What actually happens? What happens to me? Make it concrete. I've no idea, so I feel nothing.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then comes the last slide. "Thank you to my twenty collaborators, and to an institute in Switzerland for five hundred dollars." Excuse me, the world is ending and we're doing acknowledgements?! If you tell me humanity is ending in the voice you'd use for a job interview, I can't believe you. Nobody does. You think you sound professional, but you sound inauthentic.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So tell stories. What are you seeing in your research, day to day? Why does it scare you? How did you, personally, end up caring about this stuff?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I didn't come into this field caring about AI safety. I wanted to sit in a corner and work out how these models work inside through interpretability research. I really wanted to do research! But, the people in this room talked me into understanding what was happening and worrying. I began to care about AI safety. That's my story. It's true, and, in persuading people, it beats any graph I've got.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Say what you believe. Say that it frightens you. Say what you want done. The caveats do matter, but put them in the appendix, or in the paper. They're not for this conversation or post.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I spent a decade giving the world facts, and it changed nothing. Let's not make the same mistake.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/TvdRkp6spZtSbf44H/being-right-is-not-a-strategy-make-them-feel-something#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Contra Pace on the Decline of Great Writing on LessWrong](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/p9R8JA6hygSLyMoTg/contra-pace-on-the-decline-of-great-writing-on-lesswrong)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It's a common pastime to share stories of the decline of the world, how things used to be better in the past, back when GPU clusters could be bought for the price of a loaf of bread and everyone had personally memorized the Feynman Lectures on Physics and would recite it on command (or at least the first volume, you didn't need to go </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">crazy</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This recently came to my attention again in a LessWrong post titled "</span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kmruNMEMK5uN3LH6n/notes-on-the-decline-of-lesswrong-s-great-writers" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Notes on the decline of LessWrong's great writers</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">", where Ben Pace asserts that the great writers of the past were better than those of today. In support of this thesis, he presents but a single graph about the writers who've been curated on LessWrong, and what year they signed up.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795218667/lexical_client_uploads/haudz8rth5vqvswbzqwf.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apparently this is proof of the decline of Western civilization, shows that nobody can remember Bayes Theorem, the biases have beaten out the heuristics, and the absurdity heuristic will forevermore win arguments on LessWrong. Alas, while it is a pleasant fiction for those akratic rationalists who wish to rationalize why the world around them is mediocre, the true instrumental rationalists here will find within themselves the willpower to investigate further.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Let us start by examining the above line in blue, which shows what year users joined who eventually had three (or more) posts curated.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795237832/lexical_client_uploads/m7iulafby5rvlsrwum9q.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I propose we view this as two spikes with trails. One in '09 (which, to be fair, is actually an import of '07, '08, and '09 from Overcoming Bias) at the launch of LW 1.0, and one in 2017 with the launch of LW 2.0. The decline afterwards is modestly better during LW 2.0 than LW 1.0, not worse.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However, the more relevant issue is that, importantly, the number of new users from 2009 through 2016 was static and the number of posts was decreasing, whereas the number of new users and new posts from 2017 to 2026 has been increasing! This is very different! This means that new users face much more competition for curation spots, as the number of curation spots does not increase.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795238507/lexical_client_uploads/lihaswgc9lzxdmqqpajx.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you get a post in the top 5% of posts, it is much less likely to get curated.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And the old guard are still competing for the curated slots. They write about a fifth of all posts and about a third of the top ones.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795233183/lexical_client_uploads/ddsos7iqlpszgiti4o5t.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">New users are writing bangers, but they're not getting curated as much. This could be because the mod team's judgment is that that the older stuff is better, but you would see this anyway even if the quality is the same (or slightly better).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The 2020s had many great posts that would have been curated in 2017, when there was a real dearth. Basically any essay from </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/XA2CwgqLFRGxwKi2w" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Aurelia's 2026 sequence</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> about how her breakthroughs in cryonics. Basically any essay from Matthew Watkins' 2023 sequence on </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/jkY6QdCfAXHJk3kea" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the ' petertodd' phenomenon</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (aka glitches in LLMs). Basically any essay from Habryka's 2025 sequence on the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/cegJvyiP2dBkhDh2K" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Lightcone Principles</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Or the many many curiosities, like Ben Goldhaber's delightful </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fB5pexHPJRsabvkQ2/unexpected-things-that-are-people"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unexpected Things that are People</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (which got </span><a href="https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=45877257" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">over 600 upvotes on HN</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">). Malmesbury's puzzling </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/WNpvK67MjREgvB8u8/do-bamboos-set-themselves-on-fire"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Do bamboos set themselves on fire?</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and Dynomight's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/sMMvhAc83XFu873Eo/alcohol-health-and-the-ruthless-logic-of-the-asian-flush"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alcohol, health, and the ruthless logic of the Asian flush</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. (Also </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/kpd83h5XHgWCxnv3h/why-i-don-t-believe-in-the-placebo-effect"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why I don't believe in the placebo effect</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by transhumanist_atom_understander.)</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795238527/lexical_client_uploads/s5voo9q1zvewar1iov7z.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You can see it more starkly here.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As for the more recent years, we still don't know, because it already took several years to get curations for those who have them. 70 authors have been curated thrice or more, and this graph shows how long it took them to get there.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795238060/lexical_client_uploads/hcrcbqogbupgihcs64m4.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So the curation graph was the wrong one to use. What now?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A proper analysis of the territory would start with more simple measures, and then rise to more indirect approaches.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Consider the basic activity of the site. It's very healthy!</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795224209/lexical_client_uploads/b6ltifylsrj2szqzcz2q.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More posts! More votes! More users!</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In his post, Mr Pace impugned not the general activity, but the quality of the authors. He also spoke of people building a "body of work" on the site, as opposed to just heckling about the latest AI developments.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">However the number of people investing in long sequences of content has also continued growing on LessWrong.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795225457/lexical_client_uploads/y7fefyswyfv30cpnownl.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Ben Pace would argue that these new sequences are of a wretched, lower quality. But consider. 2012 had Scott Alexander's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/ZNNi2uNx9E6iwGKKG" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Intro to Game Theory</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and 2022 had Logan Strohl's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/evLkoqsbi79AnM5sz" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Intro to Naturalism</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. 2010 had </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/ynMFrq9K5iNMfSZNg" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Living Luminously</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by Alicorn, and 2020 had Daniel Kokotajlo's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/dZMDxPBZgHzorNDTt" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Takeoff and Takeover in the Past and Future</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. 2015 had </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/pFatcKW3JJhTSxqAF" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Replacing Guilt</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by Nate Soares, 2025 had </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/cegJvyiP2dBkhDh2K" rel="noreferrer"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Lightcone Principles</span></b></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by Oliver Habryka. I am not clearly seeing the past beat out the future, in fact I think I am generally seeing more good ones now than in the past.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So what gives? Why did so many people join the ridiculous Ben Pace on his false assumption, and conjure up their own explanations for the apparent decline?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Other than a delightfully high rate of crackpots, the past had more of a sense of the beginning of a new ideology, which is thrilling, and also ideological unity, which feels kind of totalizing in a way that makes it feel like the entire world of these people is within a single frame, that is again thrilling. It is deeply sad that the epistemic framework of the sequences has not received groundbreaking additions (though I love what has been added), but I think the ideological conformity going away is substantially a positive. The downside of it is that there are more people on LessWrong who will not admit to making basic errors by the lights of the sequences. (Perhaps the other downside is that there are fewer on LessWrong who are doing anything as deeply ambitious?)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But nonetheless, new authors are consistently producing content that is strongly upvoted on LessWrong. If we look at the top 5th percentile of posts each year (e.g. this would be getting at least 150 karma this year, or at least 60 karma in 2011) we can see that new authors have continued to contribute the top tier of posts.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1795224839/lexical_client_uploads/ehx6kv741nrqu4faufn0.png" /><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Perhaps we can discuss further what else has changed in the culture, and how else we can infuse the website with more of the virtues of ambitious truth-seeking and so on that we care about.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But if there's one thing we know for sure, it's that Ben Pace is wrong.</span></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/p9R8JA6hygSLyMoTg/contra-pace-on-the-decline-of-great-writing-on-lesswrong#comments">Discuss</a>

## AI/ML

### [The US-China Open-Weight Feedback Loop](https://sebastianraschka.com/blog/2026/us-china-open-weight-feedback-loop.html)

**来源**: Sebastian Raschka

**摘要**: How US and Chinese open-weight LLMs build on each other, illustrated with a timeline of architecture ideas from 2021 to 2026.
